Familiarization Program for Independent Directors:

	Annual Performance Evaluation of the Board, its
Committees and individual Directors:
The Board, along with the NRC, approved a criteria
framework in the form of a questionnaire for annual
evaluation of the Board, Board Committees and
Individual Directors pursuant to the provisions of
the Act and the Corporate Governance requirements
under Regulation 25(4) of the SEBI Listing
Regulations read with SEBI’s Guidance Note on
Board Evaluation.

The Company has a program in place to familiarize
its Independent Directors. The program’s primary
objective is to familiarize Independent Directors on
our Board with the Company’s business, industry
in which the Company operates, business model,
challenges and so on, through a variety of programs
that include regular Meetings with our business
leads and functional heads, as well as interaction
with subject matter experts within the Company.

During the year under review, the Board of Directors
has carried out an annual evaluation of its own
performance, Board Committees and Individual
Directors. The aforementioned annual performance
evaluation was carried out through a digitized
questionnaire-based survey administered via a
secure electronic portal. The evaluation framework
covered qualitative parameters and sought feedback
through ratings.

The familiarization program and other disclosures
as specified under the SEBI Listing Regulations is
available on the Company’s website at https://www.
hitachienergy.com/in/en/investor-relations/boardof-directors.

The evaluation questionnaire/ survey was
circulated electronically to all the Board members
of the Company in a transparent and confidential
manner. The key parameters considered for Board
evaluation were Board Membership, Board’s
Culture and Relationships with Key Constituencies,
Board Responsibilities, Decision Making and Board
Committees. During the evaluation process, the
Directors have given ratings of either ‘Strongly
Agree’ / ‘Agree’ on various assessment questions,
through the portal.

	Selection and Procedure for Nomination and
Appointment of Directors and Nomination and
Remuneration Policy of the Company:
The Nomination and Remuneration Committee
(‘NRC’) of the Company is entrusted to determine
the criteria for the requirements of the Board. NRC
while recommending candidatures to the Board,
takes into consideration the qualification, attributes,
experience and independence of the candidate.
Pursuant to Section 178(3) of the Act, the NRC of
the Board has formulated, amongst others, a Policy
on Nomination and Remuneration which provides
the framework for remunerating the members of
the Board, KMP, Senior Management and other
employees of the Company. This Policy is guided
by the principles and objectives enumerated in
Section 178(4) of the Act.
The details of the Nomination and Remuneration
Policy are mentioned in the report on Corporate
Governance and the same is also placed on the
Company’s website at https://www.hitachienergy.
com/in/en/investor-relations/board-of-directors.

annexed as Annexure-B to this Report.

30. WHISTLEBLOWER POLICY/ VIGIL MECHANISM:
Pursuant to Section 177(9) of the Act and
Regulation 22 of the SEBI Listing Regulations, the
Company has adopted a Whistleblower Policy/
Vigil Mechanism for Directors, Employees and third
parties to report their concerns about unethical or
inappropriate behavior, actual or suspected fraud or
violation of the Company’s Code of Conduct, leak of
unpublished price sensitive information and related
matters.
This mechanism also provides adequate safeguards
against the victimization of whistleblowers who
avail of the whistleblower / vigil mechanism.
Whistleblowers also have access to senior
management and/or the Audit Committee. The
Whistleblower Policy is available on the Company’s
website at https://www.hitachienergy.com/
in/en/about-us/integrity/reporting-channels/
whistleblower-protection-policy.
During the year under review, the Company received
a total of 18 cases, of which 14 have been closed
and 4 are currently under investigation as on date
of this report.
31. RISK MANAGEMENT POLICY:
The Company has in place the Risk Management
Policy and constituted the Risk Management
Committee as required under the Act and Regulation
21 of the SEBI Listing Regulations. The Committee is
chaired by an Independent Director, which assists the
Board in monitoring and overseeing implementation
of the Risk Management Policy, including evaluating
the adequacy of risk management systems and such
other functions as mandated under the SEBI Listing
Regulations and as the Board may deem fit from
time to time.
The Committee oversees the Risk Management
process including risk identification, impact
assessment, effective implementation of the
mitigation plans and risk reporting. The purpose of
the Committee is to assist the Board of Directors in
fulfilling its oversight responsibilities with regard to
enterprise risk management.

and Redressal) Act, 2013 and the Rules made
thereunder that mandates no tolerance against any
conduct amounting to sexual harassment of women
at the workplace.
The Company has also constituted an Internal
Complaints Committee (‘ICC’) for reporting and
conducting inquiries into the complaints made by the
victim on harassment at the workplace. Throughout
the year, training and awareness events are held
to instill sensitivity towards creating a respectful
workplace.
During the financial year under review, no complaints
pertaining to sexual harassment of women
employees were received. Further, the Company has
a web portal known as “Hitachi Energy Ethics Web
Portal” wherein employees can report/ raise interalia the workplace harassment concerns/ related
incidents. The sexual harassment complaints as
received via this Portal were investigated / being
investigated and brought to the attention of the
Audit Committee of the Board from time to time.

Annexure-A to Board’s Report
Management Discussion & Analysis

The geopolitical dimension of energy is now intersecting
with another defining force of this time - technology.
India’s digital economy is scaling rapidly. Artificial
intelligence, cloud computing and data centers are
fueling electricity demand. Unlike traditional industries,
these sectors require uninterrupted quality power. In this
context, energy is becoming a competitive differentiator.
India is clearly on route to gradually emerging as an
‘electrostate’.
The Government’s continued focus on strengthening
the power sector through policy support, infrastructure
development initiatives and measures aimed at
accelerating the energy transition, has created a
conducive environment for long-term sectoral growth.
Trade and economic cooperation agreements with
global partners are also supporting technology access,
investment flows and the development of a more
integrated energy ecosystem.

Graph 1
6.8-7.2%

Rising manufacturing capabilities, digitalization,
transportation electrification, infrastructure expansion
and urbanization continue to shape investments across
the power value chain. At the same time, efforts to
diversify energy sources and strengthen domestic
potential are supporting the country’s broader objective
of enhancing energy security and reducing exposure to
external disruptions.

The outlook for the Indian economy remains positive
and stable, with real GDP growth. GDP grew by 7.6% in
FY 2025-26 vis-à-vis 7.1% in FY 2024-25, supported
by strong domestic demand, income tax exemptions, GST
rationalization and an accommodative monetary policy.
In a global outlook increasingly shadowed by the conflict
in West Asia, the International Monetary Fund (IMF)
positions India as one of the few resilient economies.

This year marked a perceptible inflection point in the
Indian economy – a dynamic geopolitical environment,
exponential growth in the energy requirements of the
country and a supportive regulatory and policy ecosystem
put energy security at the heart of economic growth.

expand and economic activity broadens across sectors,
the need for reliable, efficient and sustainable power

support long-term economic development.

India’s energy transition agenda remains centered on
expanding the share of cleaner energy sources while
ensuring a reliable and affordable power supply. As the
energy mix evolves, greater emphasis is being placed on
transmission infrastructure, grid modernization, energy
storage and digital technologies to support system
flexibility and maintain grid stability.

Sustained government infrastructure expenditure,
alongside steadily rising private sector investments, will
anchor this expansion, complemented by a strengthening
manufacturing foundation. The services sector anticipates
continued consistent performance, reinforcing structural
economic resilience.

security and decarbonization, India continues to
strengthen the foundations of a more sustainable and
resilient energy ecosystem. These developments are

transformation, shaped by a powerful convergence of
sustained GDP growth, structural demand expansion,
progressive policy support and an accelerating
sustainability agenda.

Indian power sector development 1,2,3
India’s power sector is undergoing a once-in-ageneration transformation, driven by structural demand
growth, policy acceleration and a decisive shift toward
electrification and decarbonization. The sector today
represents a large and expanding total addressable
market (TAM), spanning generation, transmission and
distribution networks, emerging technologies and enduse industries.
FY 2025–26 marked a defining year for the sector,
reflecting both resilience and rapid structural progress.
India successfully met a peak power demand of 242.49
GW, while energy shortages declined sharply to 0.03%,
indicating strong supply reliability. Power availability has
improved significantly across the country – rural supply
increased from 12.5 hours in 2014 to 22.6 hours in
2025, while urban areas now enjoy over 23 hours of
daily supply, highlighting near-universal access and
enhanced service quality.
Two major milestones underscore the scale of
this transformation:

•

Total installed power capacity surpassed 520
GW as of March 2026 – more than doubling
from 249 GW in 2014.

•

Renewable energy crossed 51% of electricity
demand at peak levels, marking a significant
step toward a cleaner and more resilient
energy system.

These achievements reflect sustained policy support,
large-scale investments and coordinated execution
across the power ecosystem.
India is firmly on the path to becoming a global energy
leader with its groundbreaking initiatives such as universal
electrification, enhanced rural power availability and the
adoption of cutting-edge technologies.
Generation, including renewable energy6
The generation segment continues to be the cornerstone
of India’s power sector, underpinning the country’s
economic growth and rising energy demand. Over the
past decade, the sector has witnessed sustained capacity
addition alongside a structural shift in its energy mix.
While thermal power remains essential for providing
baseload stability and ensuring grid reliability, the growth
trajectory of the sector is increasingly being shaped by
renewable energy, particularly solar and wind, driven by
strong policy support, competitive auction mechanisms
and improving cost economics.
India’s electricity generation has expanded significantly
from 1,168 billion units (BU) in FY 2015–16 to 1,845.9
BU in FY 2025–26, reflecting robust demand growth
across industrial, commercial and residential segments. In
parallel, the country’s installed power capacity increased
from 305 GW to 520 GW over the same period,
highlighting the scale of infrastructure development
and investment across the generation value chain. This
expansion has been broad-based, with a clear increase
in the contribution of non-fossil fuel sources, indicating a
gradual but decisive transition toward a more diversified
and sustainable energy mix.

Source3: PIB

© 2026 Hitachi Energy. All rights reserved.

As of FY 2025–26, India’s installed capacity profile
reflects this evolving structure. While coal continues
to account for the largest share at 42.5%, renewable
energy sources have significantly strengthened their
position, with solar contributing 28.7% and wind
10.5% of the total capacity. Hydro, nuclear and other
sources together provide additional diversification. This
changing composition underscores the dual approach
being followed by the sector – maintaining conventional
capacity for stability while rapidly scaling up clean energy
to meet long-term sustainability objectives.

This transition is even more evident in the generation mix.
Total power generation in FY 2025–26 stood at 1,845.9
BU, with fossil fuels contributing 1,306.9 BU, or 70.8%
of the total and non-fossil sources accounting for 538.9
BU, or 29.2%. Notably, while fossil fuel-based generation
declined by 4.1% year-on-year, non-fossil generation
grew by 15.7%, driven by strong growth across renewable
segments. Solar and wind generation increased by over
20% and 27% respectively, while hydro and bioenergy
also recorded steady growth. This divergence reflects a
clear structural shift, with renewable energy increasingly
contributing to incremental generation.

Source4: PIB

The Rising Share of Renewable Energy: India at the
Forefront of the Global Transition
A new energy paradigm is unfolding globally, with
renewable energy rapidly increasing its share in the
power mix, driven by decarbonization imperatives, energy
security concerns and technological advancements.
Within this global transition, India has emerged as one
of the most dynamic and influential markets, combining
scale, speed and policy ambition to redefine its
energy landscape.
India’s renewable energy journey is gaining unprecedented
momentum, supported by strong government push, rising
electricity demand and a strategic focus on sustainability
and energy independence. The country recorded a
landmark addition of 55.29 GW of renewable capacity in
FY 2025–26, underscoring the accelerating shift toward
clean energy and positioning renewables at the center of
its power sector transformation.
This rapid expansion has firmly established India as a
global clean energy leader, ranking 3rd in solar power
capacity, 4th in wind power and 4th in overall renewable
energy capacity globally. As of March 31, 2026, India’s
total installed power capacity stood at 520 GW, of which
283.46 GW was contributed by non-fossil fuel sources,
including approximately 274 GW from renewable energy.
The renewable portfolio is led by 150.26 GW of solar
capacity, followed by 56.09 GW of wind power and over
51 GW of hydro power, along with contributions from
bioenergy and small hydro.
This structural shift reflects the increasing dominance of
clean energy within India’s power system. Renewables
are expected to overtake coal-fired generation as
the leading source of electricity as early as CY 2025
or CY 2026, marking a historic inflection point in the
country’s energy mix. During FY 2025–26, India also
achieved its highest-ever renewable share in electricity
generation, with renewables meeting over 51% of total
power demand at peak levels, highlighting both capacity
scale and system integration capability.
At a global level, the transition is equally pronounced.
Wind and solar generation surpassed 5,000 TWh in CY
2025 and is expected to exceed 6,000 TWh in CY 2026,
with solar PV accounting for nearly 80% of incremental
additions. The combined share of solar and wind in
global electricity generation is projected to rise from
around 15% in CY 2024 to 20% by CY 2026, reflecting
the fastest growth in any energy segment over the
past decade.
India’s progress in renewable energy is not only about
capacity expansion but also about achieving broader

Graph3: ICED

Integrated Annual Report 2025-26

164

STAT U TO RY R E P O RT S

strategic objectives. The increasing share of non-fossil
fuel sources signals a decisive move toward reducing
fossil fuel dependency, strengthening energy security
and building a more resilient energy framework.
Importantly, this transition has been achieved alongside
rising electricity demand, demonstrating the robustness
and scalability of India’s energy system.
The country’s achievements are also reflected in its
climate commitments. India has already met one of its
key COP26 (Glasgow, 2021) “Panchamrit” targets – to
achieve 50% of installed power capacity from non-fossil
fuel sources by 2030 – five years ahead of schedule.
This early milestone underscores the effectiveness of
sustained policy support, strong institutional coordination
and large-scale investment across the energy value chain.
In parallel, India continues to strengthen its energy
resilience. As the third-largest producer and consumer of
electricity globally, the country plays a central role in the
global energy transition, with its share in global primary
energy demand expected to rise significantly over the next
decade. Improvements in power availability, reduction in
shortages – from 4.2% in FY 2013–14 to near-negligible
levels in recent years—and near-universal electrification
have reinforced the reliability and inclusiveness of the
power system.
Overall, the increasing share of renewable energy in India’s
power mix reflects a deeper structural transformation. It
highlights the country’s ability to align economic growth
with sustainability imperatives, scale clean energy at an
unprecedented pace and build a resilient, future-ready
energy ecosystem.
India is not only participating in the global energy
transition – it is actively shaping it.
Transmission: Enabling the energy transition 5,6,7
The transmission sector is emerging as a critical enabler
of India’s energy transition, poised for significant scaleup. This growth is underpinned by rising peak power
demand, rapid renewable energy additions and the
targets outlined in the National Electricity Plan (NEP)
2022–32. As renewable capacity expands – often in
resource-rich yet remote locations – it is driving the
need for substantial investments in high-capacity, longdistance transmission corridors.
Structural enablers such as Green Energy Corridors,
stronger grid interconnections and enhanced crossregional transfer capabilities are expanding the
addressable opportunity. At the same time, the sector is
seeing increasing emphasis on grid reliability, efficiency
and digitalization. The Tariff-Based Competitive Bidding
(TBCB) framework has further transformed the sector,

Source5: Eninrac

165

Source6: IEEFA

Structural enablers such as Green
Energy Corridors, stronger grid
interconnections and enhanced
cross-regional transfer capabilities are
expanding the addressable opportunity.

driving competitive intensity, improving project quality
and accelerating execution timelines.
The sector recorded robust growth in FY 2025–26,
led by large-scale renewable energy evacuation
projects and continued strengthening of both the InterState Transmission System (ISTS) and state networks.
Significant commissioning of 765 kV and 400 kV
corridors connected key renewable hubs such as Khavda
(Gujarat) and Bhadla (Rajasthan) to major demand
centers. Notably, over 2,300 km of 765 kV transmission
lines were commissioned during the year, reinforcing the
backbone of India’s high-capacity national grid.
While government initiatives such as the Green Energy
Corridor (GEC) and the General Network Access (GNA)
framework have accelerated transmission buildout,
there remains scope to further fast-track greenfield
development through structured Public-Private
Partnership (PPP) models. Such approaches can enable
more effective risk-sharing and faster execution.
These efforts are aligned with the government’s
“One Grid, One Nation” vision, aimed at building a
unified, resilient and seamlessly integrated national
transmission network.
Distribution: Reforms and efficiency imperative
The distribution segment remains central to the sector’s
value chain, with ongoing reforms aimed at improving
operational efficiency and financial sustainability. Smart
metering, loss reduction initiatives and regulatory
interventions are transforming the segment. As
consumption scales and end-user expectations evolve,
distribution utilities are emerging as key drivers of
demand visibility and service innovation.
Energy storage: A new growth frontier
Energy storage is rapidly emerging as one of the
most compelling growth segments within the power
ecosystem, playing a critical role in addressing renewable
energy intermittency and enhancing grid reliability.
Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) are witnessing
accelerated deployment driven by falling costs, policy
support and the need for flexible, fast-response capacity.

Source7: Report by Avener Capital

© 2026 Hitachi Energy. All rights reserved.

At the same time, Pumped Storage Projects (PSPs)
are gaining renewed momentum as viable large-scale,
long-duration storage solutions, particularly suited for
balancing peak demand and supporting high renewable
penetration. In parallel, green hydrogen is evolving as a
strategic pillar in the energy transition, offering significant
potential for industrial decarbonization and positioning
India as a future export hub.
Mobility: Rail, Metro and Electric vehicles
The electrification of mobility is emerging as a powerful
structural driver of electricity demand in India. Large-scale
initiatives such as Indian Railways’ ongoing electrification
and the rapid expansion of metro rail networks across
urban centers are significantly increasing the reliance on
electric power for transportation.
In parallel, the accelerating adoption of electric vehicles
(EVs) is creating a new and fast-growing demand
segment. The build-out of EV charging infrastructure
represents a scalable and distributed load category,
unlocking opportunities across the entire power value
chain—from generation and transmission to distribution
and smart grid management.
As mobility transitions toward cleaner energy sources, it
is not only driving incremental demand but also reshaping
load patterns, necessitating investments in grid flexibility,
storage integration and digital energy management
solutions. This evolving landscape positions the power
sector as a central enabler of sustainable mobility and
long-term decarbonization.
Data centres: The digital demand multiplier

8,9,10

The rapid growth of data centers is introducing a
structurally new and high-intensity demand segment
within the power sector. Driven by accelerating digitization,
cloud adoption and the rise of AI-led workloads, data
centers are evolving into one of the fastest-growing
electricity consumers globally and in India.
India’s data center market is estimated at USD 11.76
billion, growing from USD 10.11 billion in CY2025 and
is projected to expand at a CAGR of 16.3% to reach USD
25.07 billion by 2031. In parallel, IT load capacity is
expected to increase sharply from ~4.5 GW in 2025 to
~12.5 GW by 2030, reflecting a robust ~22.7% CAGR
over the period.
This growth is translating into substantial power demand
expansion. Installed data center power capacity is
expected to double from ~1 GW currently to ~2 GW
by 2026 and further scale up to over 8 GW by 2030,
implying a near five-fold increase. As a result, data
centers are projected to account for ~3% of India’s total

Source8: Marketresearch

electricity consumption by 2030, up from ~1% today
underscoring their rising significance as a demand driver.
From an infrastructure perspective, India already hosts
~150 operational data centers with 1.2–1.5 GW capacity,
having attracted investments of approximately USD 6.5
billion over the past decade. Policy support remains a
key enabler, with the government granting infrastructure
status to the sector and introducing incentives such as a
21-year tax holiday for foreign cloud service providers in
Budget 2026–27, alongside favourable transfer pricing
frameworks. A long-term policy roadmap extending to
2047 further reinforces India’s ambition to become a
global hub for AI and cloud infrastructure.
Importantly, data centers are highly energy-intensive –
often consuming power comparable to large industrial
facilities or up to 1,00,000 households per facility –
creating localized, high-density load centers. This not only
drives incremental demand but also necessitates reliable,
high-quality and increasingly green power solutions.

Exports: Expanding regional and global footprint

Power trading: Market evolution and efficiency

India is increasingly positioning itself as a global hub
for power equipment manufacturing, engineering
services and project execution. Export opportunities are
expanding across key segments such as transformers,
grid technologies and EPC services, supported by strong
cost competitiveness, a skilled workforce and steadily
improving technological capabilities.

Power trading is playing an increasingly important role
in enhancing market efficiency and balancing supplydemand dynamics. The continued growth of power
exchanges, real-time markets and ancillary service
mechanisms is enabling better price discovery, improved
liquidity and optimized grid operations.

In parallel, cross-border power trade is gaining
momentum, strengthening India’s role within the regional
energy ecosystem. Power exports to neighboring
countries are fostering energy security, enhancing
grid integration and creating a more interconnected
South Asian power market. Collectively, these trends
are enabling India to expand its global footprint while
unlocking incremental growth avenues for the sector.
Services: Lifecycle value creation
Beyond core infrastructure development, services are
emerging as a critical and growing component of the
power sector’s value pool. Lifecycle services – including
operations and maintenance (O&M), digital monitoring,
predictive analytics, performance optimization and asset
management – are gaining increasing traction.
As utilities and developers focus on maximizing efficiency,
reliability and returns over the asset life cycle, service-led
engagement models are becoming more prominent. This
shift not only drives recurring revenue streams but also
deepens customer relationships and improves long-term
asset performance, making services a key strategic lever
in the evolving power ecosystem.

With rising renewable energy penetration, the need for
flexible and dynamic trading frameworks is becoming
more critical. These mechanisms help manage
intermittency, ensure grid stability and facilitate the
efficient integration of diverse energy sources. As the
market matures, power trading is set to become a
central pillar in driving efficiency and resilience across
the energy system.
Policy and regulatory environment: driving energy
transition and sector transformation
The Indian power sector continues to witness strong
policy momentum, with the Government implementing
a comprehensive set of reforms and targeted initiatives
in FY 2025–26 to accelerate the energy transition,
strengthen infrastructure and enhance sector efficiency.
These measures are collectively shaping a more resilient,
sustainable and self-reliant power ecosystem.
Renewable energy and energy transition remain at the
core of policy focus. Capacity additions in renewable
energy have remained robust, with clean sources
contributing a dominant share of incremental capacity.
Key government programs such as PM Surya Ghar:
Muft Bijli Yojana (for rooftop solar adoption) and the

Graph4: Electricity 2026: India’s demand surge, grid strain and shifting power mix | Power Peak Digest

167

© 2026 Hitachi Energy. All rights reserved.

w
PM-KUSUM Scheme (for solarization of agriculture) are
driving distributed renewable growth, while the Solar
Parks Scheme continues to enable large-scale utility
projects. The PLI Scheme for High Efficiency Solar
PV Modules is strengthening domestic manufacturing
capabilities and initiatives such as PM JANMAN are
expanding solar access to underserved and remote
communities, supporting inclusive energy access.
To further reinforce the ecosystem, the Government has
launched the National Manufacturing Mission for Clean
Technologies, aimed at building domestic capabilities
across solar modules, batteries, wind equipment and
electrolysers. This policy push aligns with the broader
objective of reducing import dependence, strengthening
supply chain resilience and supporting India’s long-term
clean energy ambitions.
Energy storage and supply chain strengthening represent
a critical next phase in the transition. Policy measures
such as the extension of Basic Customs Duty (BCD)
exemptions for Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS)
are expected to reduce capital costs and accelerate
deployment of grid-scale storage. This is essential to
manage renewable intermittency, improve grid stability
and enable higher penetration of clean energy.

participation. These initiatives aim to create clean
baseload capacity and build future-ready energy systems
aligned with India’s long-term climate goals.
Thermal sector reforms and energy security continue to
evolve under the revised SHAKTI Policy, which enhances
flexibility in coal allocation and broadens eligibility for
linkages. This is improving the availability of domestic
coal, reducing dependence on imports and exposure to
global price volatility and ensuring more stable and costefficient baseload generation.
Finally, foundational reforms and sector progress
– including flagship programs such as DDUGJY
and SAUBHAGYA – have enabled near-universal
electrification, significantly improving access to power
and reducing supply deficits. These efforts continue
to underpin demand growth and support overall
sector stability.

Overall, the policy environment
remains strongly supportive,
with a clear emphasis on clean
energy transition, infrastructure
strengthening, financial sustainability
and energy security, creating a robust
foundation for sustained growth in the
power sector.

Transmission and distribution reforms continue to be a key
priority area. The Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme
(RDSS), with enhanced allocation of approximately
` 18,000 Crores in FY 2025-26, is supporting:

•

Reduction of Aggregate Technical & Commercial
(AT&C) losses

•

Large-scale rollout of smart metering

•

Financial and operational turnaround of DISCOMs

In addition, the Government is incentivizing states through
reform-linked borrowing mechanisms, while significantly
scaling investments in inter-state and intra-state
transmission networks to facilitate renewable evacuation.
These interventions are improving operational efficiency,
enhancing transparency and strengthening reliability
across the distribution segment.
At a structural level, reforms such as the Draft National
Electricity Policy (NEP) 2026 and ongoing distribution
sector reforms (including proposed amendments to the
Electricity Act) are providing a long-term roadmap for the
sector. These focus on resource adequacy planning, tariff
rationalization, reduction of cross-subsidies and deeper
integration of renewable energy and storage, while
promoting competition and improving service quality.
Decarbonization and emerging technologies are being
actively supported through flagship initiatives such as
the National Green Hydrogen Mission, incentives for
biogas and alternative fuels and an enhanced focus
on nuclear energy, including the development of Small
Modular Reactors (SMRs) with potential private sector

Integrated Annual Report 2025-26

Opportunities and threats
The Indian power sector presents significant
growth opportunities, underpinned by strong policy
support, structural demand drivers and the ongoing
energy transition.
•

Energy transition and renewable integration

	India’s commitment to achieving 900 GW of nonfossil capacity by 2035 and net-zero emissions by
2070 is driving large-scale investments in renewable
energy. This transition is creating strong demand for
advanced grid integration solutions, including HVDC,
FACTS and grid automation technologies, as the
share of intermittent renewable energy increases.
•

Expansion and modernization of transmission
infrastructure

	Continued investments in Green Energy Corridors,
interstate transmission expansion and grid
strengthening initiatives are driving sustained
demand for high-voltage transmission systems.
Additionally, aging infrastructure and rising peak
demand are accelerating opportunities in digital
substations, flexible transmission and grid upgrades.

168

STAT U TO RY R E P O RT S

•

Energy storage and grid flexibility

	Increasing renewable penetration is creating a
structural need for battery energy storage systems
(BESS), pumped storage and grid balancing
solutions. Policy support, including incentives
and duty rationalization, is expected to accelerate
adoption, opening new avenues in hybrid solutions
and grid stabilization technologies.
•

Digitalization of power networks

	Utilities are increasingly adopting smart grid
technologies, advanced analytics and real-time
monitoring systems to enhance efficiency and
reduce losses. This is driving demand for softwareled solutions, automation and lifecycle services,
enabling long-term value creation.
•

Electrification, urbanization and emerging demand
segments

	Rapid urbanization, industrial expansion and
rising per capita consumption continue to drive
electricity demand. New segments such as EV
stands for electric vehicle not electric mobility.
Either keep electric mobility or electric vehicle, rail
electrification, metro systems and data centres are
creating high-growth, quality-sensitive demand for
reliable power infrastructure.
•

Localization and manufacturing opportunities

	Government initiatives such as PLI schemes
and the National Manufacturing Mission for
Clean Technologies are strengthening domestic
manufacturing ecosystems across solar, batteries
and grid equipment. This supports localization,
improves cost competitiveness and enhances
export potential.
•

New energy
technologies

vectors

and

decarbonization

	Emerging areas such as green hydrogen, nuclear
energy (including SMRs) and carbon markets
are creating long-term growth opportunities and
expanding the sector’s addressable market.
Turning challenges into innovation opportunities
Sector challenges such as renewable intermittency,
transmission losses and grid inefficiencies are creating
opportunities for smart grid solutions, storage
technologies and advanced energy management
systems, enabling innovation-led growth.
Threats
Despite strong growth in drivers, the sector faces
structural and emerging challenges that may impact
execution and profitability:

169

•

DISCOM financial stress

	Persistent issues such as high AT&C losses, delayed
payments and tariff distortions continue to affect
liquidity across the value chain, posing counterparty
and receivables risks.
•

Regulatory and policy uncertainty

	Evolving regulatory frameworks, tariff mechanisms
and tendering processes may create uncertainty
in project execution and investment planning,
impacting timelines and returns.
•

Project execution and counterparty risks

	Large infrastructure projects are exposed to land
acquisition delays, permitting challenges and
contractor performance risks. The financial condition
of utilities may also affect project realization.
•

Supply chain bottlenecks

	Limited availability of critical components like steel,
copper, bushings, semiconductors etc can delay
planned capacity additions, impacting revenue
and margins
•

Renewable intermittency and grid stability risks

	The growing share of renewable energy introduces
challenges in grid balancing, forecasting and
storage availability, requiring significant investments
in flexibility and system resilience.
•

Foreign exchange and macroeconomic risks

	Fluctuations in currency, interest rates and inflation
may impact project economics and profitability,
particularly for projects involving imported
components or global contracts.
•

Thermal transition and energy security challenges

	While coal remains critical for baseload supply,
evolving environmental norms and decarbonization
pressures may require additional investments and
asset realignment.
•

Cybersecurity and digital risks

	Increasing digitalization exposes grid infrastructure to
cybersecurity threats and operational vulnerabilities,
making system resilience a critical priority.
•

Talent and capability constraints

	The transition toward digital and advanced
technologies requires specialized skills in
engineering, analytics and project execution and
talent shortages may pose execution challenges.
India represents a high-growth, structurally attractive
market driven by electrification, renewable integration
and grid modernization. While the long-term outlook
remains robust, effective management of regulatory
complexity, execution challenges and financial risks will
be critical to delivering sustainable and profitable growth.

Outlook 12,13,14
Overall, the sector is well-positioned for sustained longterm growth, underpinned by a balanced focus on energy
security, clean energy transition and infrastructure
development, reinforcing its role as a key enabler of
economic growth and sustainability

all. India’s energy journey over the past decade reflects
a strategic shift towards self-reliance, sustainability and
innovation. With bold reforms, massive infrastructure
expansion and a strong push for renewables, the country
is not only meeting its growing energy demands but also
contributing meaningfully to global climate goals.

Despite economic headwinds, the electrification of
transport, industry and cooling is expected to continue
driving growth in electricity demand. Policymakers and
energy stakeholders will address supply security, pricing
volatility and grid stability to sustain the momentum
towards global energy transition goals.

From green hydrogen to solar rooftops, rural
electrification to digital procurement, every initiative
underscores India’s vision of inclusive, secure and clean
energy for all. As the nation moves forward, its balanced
and forward-looking energy strategy continues to power
economic growth and a sustainable future.

The World Energy Outlook 2025 (WEO-2025) warns
that energy security is now inseparable from geopolitics,
supply chain resilience and climate stability. It also stated
that emerging economies, particularly India and Asia, will
shape the next era of global demand.

India’s power sector outlook is underpinned by:

•

Per capita demand catch-up: At ~1.42 MWh, India’s
per capita consumption is well below global levels
(~3.75 MWh), suggesting potential to nearly double
by 2035 as demand rises.

Renewable sources of electricity generation continue to
grow strongly, with global capacity expected to more than
double by CY 2030. In 80% of the countries worldwide,
renewable power capacity is set to grow faster between
CY 2025 and CY 2030 than in the previous five-year
period. Renewables are expected to meet over 90% of
global electricity demand growth, becoming the largest
source of electricity generation globally and contributing
46% of global electricity by CY 2030.

•

Tech-led growth: EVs and AI/data center applications
could add tens of TWh to demand by CY 2030–32.

•

Green energy targets: With a 500 GW renewables
ambition by CY 2030, India will increasingly pivot
to clean sources, improving energy security and
emissions profile.

In India, the ongoing expansion of renewable energy
capacity is supported by long-term policy measures and
infrastructure planning initiatives. The Government’s
plans for a transmission network capable of supporting
900 GW of non-fossil fuel capacity by FY 2035-36 reflect
the scale of the country’s energy transition ambitions.
As renewable energy penetration increases, maintaining
grid stability, minimizing renewable energy curtailment
and managing system-level challenges such as lowfrequency oscillations will assume greater importance,
necessitating continued investments in transmission
infrastructure, grid modernization, energy storage and
advanced grid management technologies.
Marked by volatile fossil fuel prices and a rapid transition
in renewables, the global energy sector impacts India
by straining its import-dependent economy. It is
simultaneously driving an ambitious, rapid expansion
of domestic renewable energy capacity. As the world’s
third-largest oil consumer, India continues to benefit
from the shifting global clean energy investments, while
facing high inflation risks from price shocks.
India is not just increasing its energy capacity, it’s reshaping
it, tackling climate change head-on while ensuring
energy security and providing modern energy access to

Company overview:
Hitachi Energy India Limited (referred to as “the
Company”) is a subsidiary of Hitachi Energy Ltd.,
headquartered in Switzerland, a global technology leader
committed to advancing the energy transition through
innovative solutions in electrification, grid infrastructure
and digitalization.
The Company is among India’s leading power technology
players, serving utility, industry and infrastructure
customers through a comprehensive portfolio of
products, projects and services across the power value
chain. Incorporated in 2019 and headquartered in
Bengaluru, it supports the transmission, distribution and
integration of electricity through technologies that enable
a more sustainable, flexible and secure energy system.
Hitachi Energy is committed to making the world’s
energy system more sustainable, secure, resilient and
affordable, enabling greater access to electricity and
empowering societies to thrive. As the Company stands

Source14: Energy

170

STAT U TO RY R E P O RT S

at the forefront of the emerging electricity era, it is aligned
with the global movement “The Next 25,” reflecting a
commitment to future generations who will inherit the
energy systems being built today.
With 19 manufacturing facilities across 8 locations in
India, supported by 13 sales offices and 8 additional
sales touchpoints and an employee strength of 2,777
(as on March 31, 2026), the Company delivers
innovative solutions across the utility, industry and
infrastructure sectors.
The Company offers wide range of products and services
for energy networks. Its solutions ensure reliable and
high-quality power supply across transmission networks,
utilities, transport systems, renewable energy, data
centers and industrial applications.
In India, the Company powers 9 out of 18 HVDC links,
while its transformers support 1 in 3 Indian Railways
locomotives. With market-leading positions across its
business units, its technologies connect over 60 GW
of renewable energy and approximately 80% of metro
systems in the country operate using its solutions. The
Company continues to drive innovation by pioneering
advanced technologies while maintaining and
modernizing one of the world’s largest installed bases.
The Company operates on a sustainable business
model, leveraging advanced technologies and digital
transformation to support customers in achieving their
Net Zero targets. It plays a key role in enhancing the
efficiency of India’s energy value chain and improving
energy accessibility, contributing to the nation’s transition
toward a carbon-neutral future. With a strong customercentric approach, the Company focuses on profitable
growth by continuously upgrading existing offerings
and introducing new products aligned with evolving
market needs.
Business segments
Core segments
The Company operates through five business units: Grid
Automation, Grid Integration, Transformers, High Voltage
Products and Service. It serves a diverse customer base
across renewables, transmission, utilities, data centers and
railways. By leveraging Service, Exports and Digitalization
as key growth drivers, while maintaining a strong focus
on operational efficiency, the Company continues to
enhance productivity, strengthen quality standards and
expand its presence in high-growth segments.
Customer-centric approach
Customer centricity remains a cornerstone of the
Company’s strategy. Guided by collaboration and cocreation, Hitachi Energy works closely with customers
and partners to advance sustainable energy solutions
across the value chain.

171

The Company actively engages with stakeholders through
customer interactions and knowledge-sharing platforms
designed to strengthen relationships, gather feedback
and enhance overall customer experience. Its front-end
sales teams, supported by technical experts, deliver
tailored solutions and project support across segments.
The Company also demonstrates its thought leadership
and technological capabilities at leading industry
platforms such as the India AI Impact Summit, Bharat
Electricity Summit and India Energy Week. Strategic
engagement is further strengthened through leadership
interactions, customer workshops and on-site seminars,
enabling the Company to address evolving technology
needs and deliver impactful, solution-oriented outcomes.
Operational performance
The Company remained focused on enhancing its overall
efficiency across all spheres of work and maintained
a sharp focus on improving customer experience
through robust project implementation. The recently
commissioned India's first HVDC city center infeed
in Mumbai is a testament to the Company’s strong
on-time execution. A strong order backlog and longterm planning have sustained the Company's growth
momentum amid the volatile geopolitical landscape.
The Company effectively navigated the impact on the
industry from the recent global energy price shocks.
The year witnessed a surge in total order backlog with an
order book of ` 29,555.3 Crores as of March 31, 2026,
reflecting the evolving energy landscape worldwide and
providing revenue visibility for several quarters. The
order book was led by orders for HVDC control system
refurbishment, grid connection solutions and transformer
and disconnector supply. In terms of segments, the
major contributors were rail, metro and data centers.
The Service segment contributed 71% to the total order
book, with key service orders received from state utilities,
data centers, transmission and industry for system
refurbishment, SCADA upgrades and life cycle services.

Exports contributed 30.3% to the
total order book (w/o HVDC), as the
Company received export orders from
the US, Europe and APAC. During the
year, the Board approved an investment
of ` 2,000 Crores to set up a greenfield
large power transformers facility in
Karjan, Vadodara, Gujarat, taking the
cumulative capex to ` 4,000 Crores.

Sustainability
Hitachi Energy India Limited is at the forefront of the clean
energy tech and digital-led revolution. Your Company’s
focus and targeted actions towards sustainability
enabled it to consolidate its position as a leader in this
sector. Your Company has set specific targets on four key
areas to be achieved by 2030 – Planet, People, Peace
and Partnerships.
For FY 2025-26, the Company has taken definitive
measures which are in line with its 2030 sustainability
goals. Several initiatives have been taken on emissions
reduction, energy and water conservation and waste
reduction. The Company achieved a 74% reduction in
CO2 emissions and an 82% reduction in waste disposed
to landfill or incineration from the baseline year of 2019.
Also, 99% of the waste produced has been processed
for recycling in the same financial year.
The Company’s Halol manufacturing facility has been
certified as Water Positive following independent
verification of its Water Positive Index for FY 2025-26.
Additionally, the facilities at Halol and Mysore have
achieved the "Platinum Level" certification for Zero
Waste to Landfill.
The Company’s sustainability strategy, which consists of
three strands of action, People, Planet and Principles,
provides guidance for the future years. The Company has
set sustainability targets and commitments for 2030 and
the roadmap for transition to Net Zero by 2050.

the year, contributing to discussions on energy
transition, renewable integration, grid modernization,
power transmission reforms and the growing energy

At Hitachi Energy India Limited, safety remains paramount

this commitment through consistent actions. Upholding

Hitachi Energy actively emphasizes the importance of
timely reporting of safety incidents across its work sites.
The Company has refreshed its Health, Safety and
Environment (HSE) policy to further promote employee
well-being, ensure safe working conditions and drive
positive health outcomes. It continues to strengthen its
safety practices across all its manufacturing locations
and offices, achieving 100% on-time closure of highrisk hazard situations.

Recordable Injury Frequency Rate (TRIFR) of 0.11,
reflecting its sustained focus and disciplined execution
of safety initiatives.

Risks and Concerns

Your Company continued to reduce its risk exposure
by strengthening its risk management practices and
enhancing organizational resilience. Integrating
quantitative risk analysis into daily operations and
performance management guides informed corporate
decision-making.
Aligned with globally recognized standards, the
Company’s comprehensive risk management framework
enables proactive identification, prioritization and risk
mitigation. This approach protects shareholder interests,
supports consistent value consistently and ensures that
emerging risks are promptly addressed through effective
mitigation and continuous monitoring.
Internal control systems and their adequacy
The Company’s internal control framework safeguards
stakeholder interests and supports operations through
a multi-layered structure of oversight, directives, entityand process-level controls and management testing.
This ensures robust financial reporting and effective
risk management, aligned with the Company’s size and
complexity. An independent Internal Audit, Risk & Internal
Control team provides insights on compliance, operational
efficiency and key risks, with quarterly reporting to the
Audit Committee to drive continuous improvement.

COMPANY’S PHILOSOPHY ON CODE OF GOVERNANCE:
Your Company’s Corporate Governance philosophy is to build and manage sustainable businesses anchored by
the highest standards of honesty, transparency and accountability. Your Company aim to maximize stakeholder
value while strictly adhering to all applicable laws and regulations. Your Company believe that effective
leadership, robust policies, processes and system and a rich legacy of values form the hallmark of a world-class
governance framework.
The Company fully realizes its Shareholders’ entitlement to information on the Company’s performance and
considers itself a trustee of its Shareholders. The Company provides thorough information to its Shareholders on a
variety of subjects impacting the Company’s business and financial performance. The Company’s basic corporate
governance concept is to achieve business excellence and devote itself to growing long-term Shareholder value
while keeping all stakeholders’ requirements and interests in mind. The Company is dedicated to transparency
in all of its dealings and values corporate ethics. Good corporate governance is the basis for decision-making
and control processes.

Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (‘SEBI Listing Regulations’):

Director. None of the Directors on the Board are related to each other (inter-se). Their professional
relationship is strictly limited to the receipt of sitting fees, commission and reimbursement of expenses
incurred for attending Company Meetings. A clear distinction exists between the roles and duties of the
Chairman and that of the MD & CEO.

(g) Board’s Core Skills/ Expertise/ Competencies:
		

For effective functioning of the Board, the Company’s Board needs to have skills/expertise/competencies
in the areas of Business, Finance & Accounting, Governance/Legal and Leadership. Accordingly, for the
effective discharge of its roles and responsibilities, the Board of the Company is comprised of Directors
possessing an appropriate mix of skills, expertise and competencies in the areas of Business, Finance
& Accounting, Governance/Legal and Leadership, relevant to the Company’s business and governance
requirements. The Board collectively brings together a diverse range of professional experience, enabling
informed decisionmaking and effective oversight.

		

The Directors come from varied professional backgrounds and geographies, bringing global perspectives
and sectoral insights that support the Company’s longterm growth. The Board as a whole possesses the
requisite qualifications, experience, independence and competencies necessary to meet the Company’s
governance standards.

(h) Succession Planning for the Board and Senior Management:
		

The Company has a well-defined and robust system in place to ensure smooth transitions in leadership,
including for our Directors, Executive Director, Senior Management Team and other critical talents and
key roles.

		

This is an annual global process where employees across all levels are rated on their Potential Index in the 9
Box grid, retention risk loss impact and successors for key positions are identified. Additionally, the Company
regularly reviews talents for Senior Management and other executive officers. This process provides a
comprehensive overview of our leadership level talent inventory and capabilities, ensuring that we meet
our critical talent needs in alignment with our business drivers. The aforementioned is also reviewed by the
Nomination & Remuneration Committee annually and recommended to the Board.

programme for its Independent Directors. As part of the familiarization program, the Directors have
been periodically apprised during the Board/Committee Meetings about the amendments to the various
enactments viz., the Act and the SEBI Listing Regulations. Further, as a part of Board and Committee

internal and external perspective, to enable informed decisionmaking.

the SEBI Listing Regulations, including, in terms of Regulation 25(8) of the SEBI Listing Regulations,
Independent Directors have confirmed that they are not aware of any circumstance or situation that
exists or may be reasonably anticipated that could impair or impact their ability to discharge their duties.

Directors, the Board have confirmed that they meet the criteria of independence as mentioned under
Regulation 16(1)(b) of the SEBI Listing Regulations and Section 149(6) of the Act and that they are
independent of the management.

Regulations and the Act.

by making presentation(s) on key business developments, business & financial performance, new
strategic initiatives, financial outlook, financial reports, risk, compliance, market outlook/evolving
trends, sustainability initiatives, CSR, Human Resources, safety, key regulatory updates.

Meeting of the Independent Directors is being held every financial year. None of the Non-Independent
Directors, Members of the Management or KMP were present for this Meeting.

was held on May 14, 2025 without the presence of Non-Executive Directors/ Managing Director/
Management, to discuss the matter as required/ agreed amongst them in accordance with the

Directors. Further, during the year under review the independent directors also had independent session
with the Statutory Auditors, without the presence of the Management, to facilitate independent and
open deliberations.

(m) Board Committees:
		

In accordance with the provisions of the applicable laws, the Board has constituted various Committees and
delegated specific powers and responsibilities to them to facilitate focused and efficient decisionmaking.
Each Committee operates within its defined terms of reference and undertakes detailed deliberations on
matters within its purview before placing its recommendations before the Board.

		

The Committees function under the overall supervision of the Board and are empowered to make informed
decisions within the scope of the authority delegated to them. Committee meetings are generally held prior
to the Board Meetings and the Chairperson of each Committee apprises the Board of the deliberations,
decisions taken and recommendations made by the Committee. All such recommendations and decisions
are placed before the Board either for approval or for information, as applicable. During the financial year
under review, all recommendations made by the Committees were accepted by the Board.

		

The following statutory Committees have been constituted by the Board in compliance with the provisions
of the Act and the SEBI Listing Regulations:

		

•

(i)

Terms of Reference:
In accordance with the provisions of Section 177 of the Act and Regulation 18 of the SEBI Listing Regulations,
the Company has formed its Audit Committee, composition and terms of reference of which are in conformity
with the said provisions.
The terms of reference are briefly described below:

•

Oversight of the Company’s financial reporting process and the disclosure of its financial information to
ensure that the financial statement is correct, sufficient and credible.

•

Recommending the Board, appointment, re-appointment, replacement or removal (in the event of necessity)
of Statutory Auditors, Cost Auditors, Secretarial Auditors and/ or any other auditors including terms of their
appointment and fixation of remuneration.

•

Approval of payment to Statutory Auditors for any other services rendered by the Statutory Auditors.

•

Reviewing, with the Management, the annual financial statements and auditor’s report thereon before
submission to the Board for approval, with particular reference to:-

		

(a) Matters required to be included in the Director’s Responsibility Statement to be included in the Board’s
Report in terms of clause (c) of sub-section (3) of Section 134 of the Act.

		

(b) Changes, if any, in accounting policies and practices and reasons for the same.

		

(c)

Nomination and Remuneration Committee:

(i)

Terms of Reference:
The terms of reference and role of the Nomination and Remuneration Committee (‘NRC’) are as per the provisions
of Section 178 of the Act and Regulation 19 of the SEBI Listing Regulations which includes formulating the
criteria to:

•

Determine qualifications, positive attributes and independence of a director and recommend to the Board
a Policy, relating to the remuneration of the directors, KMP and other employees.

•

For every appointment of an Independent Director, the NRC shall evaluate the balance of skills, knowledge
and experience on the Board and on the basis of such evaluation, prepare a description of the role and
capabilities required of an Independent Director. The person recommended to the Board for appointment
as an Independent Director shall have the capabilities identified in such description. For the purpose of
identifying suitable candidates, the Committee may:

		

(a) use the services of an external agency, if required;

		

(b) consider candidates from a wide range of backgrounds, having due regard to diversity; and

		

(c)

consider the time commitments of the candidates.

•

Evaluation of performance of Independent Directors and the Board.

•

Devising a policy on Board diversity.

•

Identifying persons who are qualified to become directors and who may be appointed in Senior Management
in accordance with the criteria laid down and recommend to the Board their appointment and removal.

•

Recommending to the Board, whether to extend or continue the term of appointment of the independent
director, on the basis of the report of performance evaluation of independent directors.

•

Recommending to the Board on remuneration, Performance Bonus etc., payable to the Executive Director(s)
/ Managing Director, Commission payable to Independent Directors.

•

Recommend to the Board, all remuneration, in whatever form, payable to Senior Management.

The Policy is framed by the NRC and approved by the Board. The terms and conditions of appointment are
disclosed on the website of the Company at: https://www.hitachienergy.com/in/en/investor-relations/boardof-directors.

(iii) Remuneration Policy / Criteria for Payments to Directors / Senior Management Employees:
The Company has a credible and transparent Policy in determining and accounting for the remuneration of
the Executive / Non-Executive Independent Directors. Independent Directors’ remuneration is governed by
the external competitive environment, track record of the individuals, effective participation in the Meetings,
comparable industry standards and performance of the Company. The remuneration determined for the Executive
/ Independent Directors is subject to the recommendation of the NRC and approval of the Board.
The Nomination and Remuneration Policy covers the following aspects:

•

Lays down the criteria and terms and conditions with regard to identifying persons who are qualified to
become Directors (Executive and Non-Executive) and persons who may be appointed at Senior Management
and Key Managerial positions and to determine their remuneration.

•

Formulation of criteria for evaluation of the performance of the Board, KMP and Senior Management
Personnel as required under the law.

The Board and the NRC also reviewed the performance of the Board on the basis of the criteria such as the
contribution of the individual director to the Board and Committee Meetings like preparedness on the issues to
be discussed, meaningful and constructive contribution and inputs in Meetings, etc. In addition, the Chairman
was also evaluated on the key aspects of his role.
The Board, based on the recommendation of the NRC has adopted the performance review criteria. After the
conclusion of the period under review, the revision was carried out by the NRC in the performance evaluation
criteria. Subsequently, the NRC and the Board have reviewed the performance of the Directors and the Board
as a whole for the financial year under review.
The Non-Executive Independent Directors were compensated by way of commission for the financial year
2025-26 in the financial year 2026-27 and the criteria being their attendance in the Board / Committee
Meetings, apart from sitting fees and reimbursement of other expenses as approved by the Shareholders at the
3rd AGM held on July 22, 2022.
As per Policy, the Non-Executive Non-Independent Directors are neither paid a sitting fee nor paid any
commission.
The Independent Directors are entitled to sitting fees for attending the Board/Committee Meetings.
Sitting fees for Board and Audit Committee Meetings is ` 60,000/- per Director per Meeting and for other
Committees, the sitting fee is ` 30,000/- per Director per Meeting.
The remuneration payable to the Managing Director and CEO and Senior Management personnel including
KMP are structured as fixed and variable components. The fixed remuneration comprises salaries, perquisites
and retirement benefits and the variable component comprises annual performance bonus which is linked to
the achievement of the scorecard fixed at the beginning of the year and Long Term Incentive Plan (‘LTIP’) which
is subject to achievement of performance measures as attached to the Grant Letter issued from time to time in
line with Hitachi Energy group Policy.

Stakeholders’ Relationship Committee:
(i)

Terms of Reference:

		

In accordance with the provisions of Section 178 of the Act and Regulation 20 read with Part D of Schedule
II of the SEBI Listing Regulations, the Company has formed its Stakeholders' Relationship Committee (SRC),
composition and terms of reference of which are in conformity with the said provisions.

		

The SRC has been authorized to approve the transfer/ transmission/ transposition/ demat/ remat of shares/
deletion of name and issue of duplicate share certificates cum letter of confirmation and taking note of
Members’ grievances.

		

Following are the terms of reference of the SRC:-

		

1.

Resolving the grievances of the security holders of the Company including complaints related to
transfer/ transmission of shares, non-receipt of annual report, non-receipt of declared dividends, issue
of new/ duplicate certificates, General Meetings, etc.

		

2.

Review of measures taken for the effective exercise of voting rights by Shareholders.

		

3.

Review of adherence to the service standards adopted by the Company in respect of various services
being rendered by the Registrar & Share Transfer Agent (‘RTA’).

		

4.

Review of the various measures and initiatives taken by the Company for reducing the quantum of
unclaimed dividends and ensuring timely receipt of dividend warrants / annual reports / statutory
notices by the Shareholders of the Company.

Resolving grievances of debenture holders related to creation of charge, payment of interest/principal,
maintenance of security cover and any other covenants.

Director and CEO, Chief Financial Officer and Company Secretary to approve the transfer/ transmission/

Risk Management Committee:
(i)

Terms of Reference:		

		

To inform Board Members about the risk assessment and minimization procedures and review to ensure
that executive management controls risk by means of a properly defined framework. The Company has
formulated a Policy on Risk Management and constituted a Risk Management Committee (‘RMC’).

		

The terms of reference and Role of the RMC are as per the provisions of Regulation 21 of the SEBI Listing
Regulations which includes formulating the criteria to:

		

1)

To formulate a detailed Risk Management Policy which shall include:-

			

(a) A framework for identification of internal and external risks specifically faced by the listed entity, in
particular including financial, operational, sectoral, sustainability (particularly, ESG related risks),
information, cyber security risks, or any other risk as may be determined by the Committee.

			

(b) Measures for risk mitigation including systems and processes for internal control of identified risks.

			

(c)

Business continuity plan.

Integrated Annual Report 2025-26

190

STAT U TO RY R E P O RT S

2)

To ensure that appropriate methodology, processes and systems are in place to monitor and evaluate risks
associated with the business of the Company.

3)

To monitor and oversee the implementation of the Risk Management Policy, including evaluating the
adequacy of risk management systems.

4)

To periodically review the Risk Management Policy, at least once in two years, including by considering the
changing industry dynamics and evolving complexity.

5)

To keep the Board informed about the nature and content of its discussions, recommendations and actions
to be taken.

6)

The appointment, removal and terms of remuneration of the Chief Risk Officer (if any) shall be subject to
review by the RMC.

Further, the list of tentative risks was also revisited and adopted in consonance with the industry and market trends.
Two Meetings were held during the financial year viz., May 14, 2025 and November 03, 2025.

Corporate Social Responsibility Committee:
Pursuant to Section 135 of the Act, a Corporate Social Responsibility (‘CSR’) Committee of the Board has been
constituted.
The terms of reference and Role of the CSR Committee are as per the provisions of Section 135 of the Act.
The CSR Committee shall be responsible for formulating and recommending to the Board the CSR Policy and
an Annual Action Plan, which shall include the following:

•

list of CSR projects or programs that are approved to be undertaken in areas or subjects as specified in
Schedule VII of the Act.

•

the manner of execution of such projects or programs.

•

the modalities of utilization of funds and implementation schedules for the projects or programs.

•

monitoring and reporting mechanism for the projects or programs.

•

details of need and impact assessment, if any, for the projects undertaken by the Company.

•

to monitor the CSR Policy of the Company from time to time.

8.

Environmental, Social and Governance Committee:
Sustainability is integral to Hitachi Energy’s business strategy and underpins the Company’s approach to
environmental stewardship, social responsibility and governance. The Company is committed to reducing its
carbon footprint, promoting employee and social welfare and upholding the highest standards of governance.
The ESG initiatives and oversight continued during the financial year under review. In accordance with
Regulation 34(2)(f) of the SEBI Listing Regulations, Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report (‘BRSR’)
covering disclosures on Company’s performance and reporting under ESG parameters for FY 2025-26,
along with Independent Assurance Statement on BRSR Core provided by SGS India Private Limited (SGS), an
independent agency forms an integral part of this Annual Report.
Charter and Objectives of ESG Committee:
Drivers of ESG:
The Company’s ESG framework is guided by multiple strategic, regulatory and stakeholder-driven factors,
including the following:

•

Hitachi Energy’s Sustainability 2030 strategy based on UN SDGs based on four key areas: Planet, People,
Peace and Partnerships

•

Investor Expectations

•

Evolving Compliance and Stock Exchange Listing Requirements

•

Contribution to an Integrated Strategic Approach-Increased revenues and innovation

•

Cost savings and efficiencies

•

Improved access to capital

•

Improvements in staff morale

•

Improved reputation and stakeholder relations

•

Doing “the right thing”

Sustainability vs ESG: ESG Factors for Better Business Performance:
Hitachi Energy has placed sustainability at the heart of its purpose to advance a sustainable energy future for all.
Company ESG Purpose and Charter Purpose:
ESG Committee has been constituted with the objective to assist the Board in fulfilling its oversight responsibilities
in relation to Hitachi Energy’s objectives, policies and practices pertaining to:

•

Environment: Interaction with the physical environment

•

Social: Impact on society and communities

•

Governance: How the company is governed

with Sustainability 2030 strategy based on four pillars of:

•

Planet - Towards carbon neutral

•

People - Diversity plus collaboration equals great innovation

•

Peace - Peaceful inclusive and sustainable societies

•

Partnerships - Foster multi stakeholder partnerships

-

The Committee shall meet at least once during the financial year. Meetings of the ESG Committee shall be
held either in person or by virtual audio/ video conference mode.

-

The Committee shall invite such other employees or engage third parties to attend the Meetings and / or
to monitor the implementation of ESG objectives.

-

The Committee shall make available to the Board the minutes of its Meetings.

Roles & Responsibilities of ESG Committee:
ESG Committee shall undertake the following:
1.

Monitor and implement ESG targets of the Company and review its adequacy.

2.

To keep the Board informed about the nature and content of its discussions, recommendations and actions
to be taken.

3.

To facilitate reporting of ESG related matters in the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report as
may be statutorily required.

4.

To perform such functions or duties as may be prescribed under the Act, the SEBI Listing Regulations and
any other applicable laws from time to time and discharge such other functions as may be specifically
delegated to the Committee by the Board from time to time.

5.

(a) The Committee shall have the authority to scrutinize programs and initiatives to ensure transparency
and oversee the implementation of programs/initiatives across the Company.

		

(b) Obtain external legal or other independent professional advice, whenever necessary.

VI. MEANS OF COMMUNICATION:
The Company recognizes communication as a key element to the overall Corporate Governance framework and
therefore emphasizes on prompt, continuous, efficient and relevant communication to all external stakeholders.
It follows a robust process of communication with its stakeholders and investors. For this purpose, it provides
multiple channels of communications, i.e., through dissemination of information on the online portal of Stock
Exchanges and placing relevant information on the website of the Company.
Financial Results:
The quarterly/ half-yearly/ annual financial results are filed with stock exchanges and displayed on stock
exchanges websites. The results are also made available on the Company’s website at https://www.hitachienergy.
com/in/en/investor-relations/financial-results. The results are published in ‘The Hindu Business Line’ (English
newspaper – all India edition) and ‘Vijaya Karnataka’ (Regional Newspaper), containing a Quick Response code
and the details of the webpage of the Company where complete financial results is accessible to the investors, as
specified in Regulation 33 of the SEBI Listing Regulations. This is pursuant to the amendment in the SEBI Listing
Regulations vide SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) (Third Amendment) Regulations, 2024
dated December 12, 2024. Additionally, all quarterly financial results, were emailed as well to the Shareholders
whose email addresses are registered with the Company/ Depository Participants (‘DP’).
Website:
The Company also utilizes its website https://www.hitachienergy.com/in/en as a valuable tool for providing
information related to its business operations and management structure. Moreover, the website also reflects
the Company’s strong commitment to sustainability initiatives and provides a dedicated section for investors
which provides comprehensive information that is required to be made available to the public.
The Investor Relations section is designed to address the information needs of various stakeholders and
enables easy access to relevant information, thereby reinforcing the Company’s commitment to transparency,
accountability and good governance, which in turn empowers its stakeholders to make informed decisions.
The financial results, press releases and the official news releases are also placed on the Company’s website at
https://www.hitachienergy.com/in/en.
Further, the Company has a dedicated e-mail ID: investors@hitachienergy.com managed by the Secretarial
Department for providing necessary information/ assistance to the Shareholders/ investors.
Press Releases, Earnings Call and Analysts/Investors’ Presentations:
The Company issues news releases to communicate important update including announcement of financial
results every quarter. Before releasing such information in press, those are submitted to the Stock Exchanges
and are also hosted on the Company’s website. The Company after announcement of results, conducts regular
earnings/ conference calls to discuss its financial performance and outlook with investors/analysts. Investors
presentations to be made at those earnings/ conference calls, to institutional investors / analysts, are beforehand
submitted to the Stock Exchanges and are hosted on the Company’s website at https://www.hitachienergy.com/
in/en/investor-relations/analyst-section.
As required under the SEBI Listing Regulations, transcripts of the said earnings/ conference call are also
submitted to the Stock Exchanges and are hosted on the Company’s website at the same link as aforementioned
i.e., https://www.hitachienergy.com/in/en/investor-relations/analyst-section.

position and various other information as required under applicable laws. The Integrated Annual Report containing
the Notice of AGM, audited standalone financial statements together with Board’s Report, Auditors’ Report and
other reports/ information, are being sent only in electronic mode to all Members whose email addresses are

Share Transfer System:
In order to enhance ease of doing investment and to further strengthen transparency and investor protection,
the Securities and Exchange Board of India (“SEBI”) has, from time to time, mandated various measures relating
to issuance, transfer and servicing of securities.
SEBI has mandated that Listed Companies shall henceforth issue the securities in dematerialized form only (vide
Gazette Notification no. SEBI/LADNRO/ GN/2022/66 dated January 24, 2022) while processing the investor
service requests mentioned in the above notification (viz., Issue of duplicate securities certificate, claim from
Unclaimed Suspense Account, Transmission, Transposition, etc.,). The transmission or transposition of shares
is being processed by the Registrar and Share Transfer Agents (‘RTA’) and approved by the SRC. In order to
expedite the process, the Board has also delegated the authority jointly to any two, of the Managing Director
(‘MD’), CFO and the Company Secretary to approve share transmission, transposition, deletion of a name, etc.,
of shares and accordingly, the same is being approved as per the requests received and the report is placed for
noting before the SRC and Board on a quarterly basis.
Further, SEBI vide its circular dated November 03, 2021 and subsequent amendments, has introduced common
and simplified norms for processing any service request received from the Shareholder related to furnishing
PAN, KYC details and Nomination by the RTA, for holders of physical securities, inter alia providing for:
1.

Electronic interface for processing investor’s queries, complaints and service requests.

2.

Mandatory furnishing of PAN, KYC details and Nomination (or opting out) by holders of physical securities.

3.

Simplification of the earlier framework relating to freezing of folios.

Pursuant to SEBI’s relaxation issued on November 17, 2023, the provision relating to freezing of folios for
nonsubmission of PAN/KYC/Nomination has been done away with. However, investors are encouraged to update
their PAN, KYC and Nomination details to enable smooth processing of service requests.
The Company through its RTA, has already sent communications to all those Shareholders whose PAN and/
or KYC details are not updated in the records of the Company and/ or RTA. Communication was sent through
registered post to all those Shareholders wherein it was requested to update the required details in line with
the aforesaid SEBI circular.
Furthermore, SEBI vide its Circular dated January 25, 2022, has made it mandatory for compulsory credit of
shares in demat form in case of the request processed for the issue of duplicate shares, claim from Unclaimed
suspense account, renewal/ exchange of securities certificate, endorsement, sub-division / splitting of securities
certificate, consolidation of securities certificates/ folios, share transfer, share transmission, share transposition
and issue of duplicate shares.
Hence, the Members holding shares in physical form are requested to consider converting their holdings in
dematerialized form. The Members who are desirous to convert their physical holdings into dematerialized form,
may contact the DP of their choice. The request for share RTA and approved by the SRC. Additionally, SEBI,
vide Circular dated January 30, 2026, has dispensed with the requirement of issuance of Letter of Confirmation
(‘LOC’) and directed that securities shall be directly credited to the demat account of the investor by the RTA /
Company after due verification, thereby significantly reducing processing timelines.
